by W. Michael Tenery III
www.role-playing.com
This is the first in a series of Imagine Bestiary Expansions. The Imagine Bestiary: Aspects
of the Wild transports the Imagine game experience into the vast unexplored territories
where wild beasts roam and the wild at heart flourish. This volume exists to expand play
for the Game Master and player alike. 
Published by Imagine Role Playing, Incorporated.  
Aspects of the Wild

===== PAGE 8 =====
iv
magine play usually begins in the homeland of the character races, with Game Master Characters as  
friends and foe. But soon the players lust for more exotic adventure, for experiencing the wilds and the 
wild things. Now Imagine game play can extend outward, into the vast unexplored territories, off the 
beaten paths. Many of Imagine's skill concepts center on the natural environment with skills like Herb 
Lore, Animal Lore, Bestiary Lore, Tame Animal, Hermetic Lore, Potion Lore, Poison Lore, Summon
Animal, Animal Shape, Animal Projection, Totem Call, Plant Shape, Control Growth and especially Evoke. When
asked what my favorite skill is in Imagine, invariably I reply Evoke, for it creates the connection between man and
beast and is the height of early mysticism as practiced by the races of Imagine. Classes such as the Druid, Shaman,
Ranger and others rely on it throughout their careers and are defined by their choice of totem.  Yet, Evoke is so
flexible that it is also defines Lycanthropy (see The Master's Manual) and when permanently applied creates the
chaotic and mysterious Famorian race. Evoking, becoming the beast in spirit, is the centerpiece of Aspects of the
Wild. 
Characters in Imagine are more likely to find themselves deep in a forbidding forest than on the 19th level of an
endless underground maze.  Aspects of the Wild brings this arena to life, adding four new classes, and many new
races, expanding the Fae dramatically and highlighting the divisions between the ordered Fae (seelie) and those
that serve chaos (unseelie). The Wild Ones have many new racial types including the chaos gifted Famorians that
seem to be many things at once and are like no other race yet seen. Of other beastfolk there are the prideful
Taurians and the cultish Beastman, the reclusive Sasquatch and the merry Satyr. All can provide new and unique
game play experiences. 
And of beasts, fair and foul there are many, from the dangerous phase tiger to the simple worm, from the falcon to
the grand griffon. Now there are new Giants of the highlands and forests, and fearsome beasts such as the spitting
hydra or the rampaging manticore. Plants, magical plants and swampy slimes rounding out the creatures featured.
Whether it is the Goblin's wyvern mount, or the Mage's pet sphixian cat, the Warrior's attack dog, or Druid's bear
friend, all can now be added to your play experience. 
A full guide of wild herbs, new spells, invocations, runes, rituals, songs, poems, recipes and more await. The
Imagine fantasy wildscape is now complete for both Game Master and player alike with the addition of this
Bestiary, Aspects of the Wild.
Credits
Developers 
W. Michael Tenery III, Chris Dodson, David Maples, and Chris Carpinello.

Ginevra Boyes, John Boyes, Chris Carpinello, Brian Carroll, Joe Carroll, Martha Carroll, George Cavander, Chris
Dodson, Eric Griffon, Virginia Huling, Scott Jarvis, David Maples, Sean Mulhall, Lon R. Rustin, Matt Simpson,
and Eric Weaver.

Brian Carroll and Chris Dodson
Edited By
Christine Corneilson 
Additional Material Submitted By: Ginevra Boyes, John Boyes, Brian Carroll, Scott Jarvis, Matt Simpson, and
Sean Mulhall.

Beastman (Ram)
A being of malevolence and fear that is both clannish and cultist, and prefers dark ways and dark gods, the Beastman
has the head and neck of a ram (both sexes) and a body somewhere between ape and human. They are fiercely tribal and
rarely seen alone. The Beastman lives in secluded caves, mountains and other remote areas. They prefer raiding as their
main resource and are often hated by other races for it. They are drawn to power and seek it no matter what the cost, but
prefer victims of other races for their sacrifices and experimentation. Females live as near slaves to the males unless they
show some dark gift or power. The Beastmans barbarism usually prevents them from being a true threat to other races.
While not known for their Wisdom, they have the intellect and ability for all but the most difficult of magic and rites. As
a race Beastman are Evil in alignment.

Brownie (Earth Fae)
Imagine a being between few inches and a foot tall, with the squeaky voice of a mouse, the bite of a rat, the curiosity of
a cat and the stubbornness of a tired mule.  Now add in a dash of magic and sprinkle of chaos and this only begins to
approximate the strange creatures that call themselves Brownies.  They are tiny nomads who wander from place to place
as the season and their unpredictable moods dictate. Their name stems both from their deeply tanned skin and their
magic that often goes amiss and burns the unfortunate. It is chiefly their curiosity that gets them into predicaments while
their stubbornness keeps them there, firming the image of a troublesome pest. Brownies know something of magic and
tend to dabble in both the seelie and the unseelie. They are quick to anger, slow to calm and are the only Fae that rou-
tinely go berserk despite the consequences. All Fae regard them as a nuisance and other races see them as downright
trouble, avoiding them where possible. As a race the Brownie tends toward Neutral.

Changeling (High Fae)
These mutable masters of form are said to be Elven in origin, but the mystery of their past is lost to time and unrecorded
lore. When not in anothers shape they appear as a thin, pale, almost milky white Elves. Their ears tend to come to a
more pronounced point, with smiles too wide, stretched over too thin lips, under wild, piebald eyes that never match and
are rarely even one whole color. The Changelings are true magpies, and are usually hidden in Elven or Human societies.
They mate only when they discover each other and after elaborate games of courtship that involve many forms and
much time.  Thus, thankfully, Changeling births are very rare. They never raise their infants. Soon after birth they steal
another child, holding their infant and the child together. The new changeling infant fixes in the childs form and is
swapped for the normal infant. These poor wretches are abandoned as far away as possible. If the normal infant is lucky
the Changeling might care enough to deposit the child with strangers rather than leave them to the wolves. The infant
Changeling stays in the new form until reaching sexual maturity, during which its power to change forms grows and is
mastered. Many changelings are caught during this time. They make horrible children that rarely mind or respect their
parents, and this has given rise to the term Changeling Child for any misbehaving youth.  Thus no race accepts
Changelings and they are feared by all common folk. They are generally a capricious and uncaring lot and are Neutral to
Neutral Evil in alignment.

Starting Age: Dark Fairies age as humans do for 21 years and
then cease aging for the remainder of their lifetime.  When a
Dark Fairy dies its body fades completely into shadow, leaving
no corporeal component.  
Maximum Age: 5000
Special Abilities/Powers: Night Vision.  Exceptional Sight.
Females can fly (per the spell Fly).  During the flight females
project mental images of wings. These wings can be anything
from a birds feathery pair, to a bats membranous ones.

Dark Fairy (Air Fae)
Touched by the hand of chaos, and turning down the left-hand path, these Fairies are dark, wild and danger-
ous, for their tricks are treacherous and sometimes fatal.  They take wicked delight in the downfall of others,
especially when they can benefit from it. They are enemies to all seelie Fae and are hunted by many races for
the magical properties of their bodies. They usually have some form of marking or pattern that marks them as
different from their Fairy origin, whether it is coal black hands, purple veins visible beneath translucent skin,
tiny horns, a stinger, furry legs or cloven feet. The Dark Fairy does deal with Gremlins and Leprechauns on
occasion but shuns all other Fae. While some Fairy and Dark Fairy societies mix, it is never without continu-
ous friction.  These beings are otherwise like their Fairy origin with different unseelie gifts. The Dark Fairy is
generally Evil.

Dryad (Earth Fae)
The Dryad is the friend of the trees, a caretaker and guardian of forested ways, although once they were
merely wild cousins of the Wood Elf before their study and some say worship of the trees began. All seelie
Fae respect and revere the Dryad for the devotion that leads them to become one with the trees, but the Wood
Elves and Gnomes are their closest allies. The Dryad understands the slow rhythms of plant lives better than
any Fae and can speak to them and harness their ancient secrets and strengths. When seen at all, they appear
as green-skinned Elves, often clad in clothes of leaves and bark.  They speak slowly and are very slow to
anger or to show any deep emotion, but they understand the long path, what the course of change can mean.
When paths diverge they can act quickly to choose a course. The Dryad can merge with a tree and often does
for months or years at a time. During this time they age very slowly and thus can lead unimaginably long
lives; some go so far as to become trees and remain so forever. Most Dryad tend toward Neutral alignments.

Famorian (Mongrel)
The mistake, the blunder, the lost children of chaos: these and other names have plagued the Famorians. They
cling to each other when they can and continue to breed and produce more Famorians. Given their disparate
parts, breeding can get down right complicated and sometimes dangerous.  Some Famorians may look very
humanoid but have a tail or tufted ears, or they can be twisted piles of odd limbs and body shapes that can
barely feed themselves. They are an accursed people who are shunned by all. As individuals they can be kind
or tormented, wise or warlike. What they always are is secretive, living in conclaves in remote locations or in
places well hidden. Their most humanoid members are used as traders and scouts for the Famorian clan. There
is a legend that the Famorians will one day breed as true humanoids again and keep only the spiritual gifts of
their animal ancestors.  Some scholars speculate that these beings are the source of all beast-like races while
others argue they are result of inbreeding between those races. While neither is certain, it is apparent that
chaos had a hand to play in their offing. It is because of this that they have a reverence for luck within their
culture and are deeply superstitious seeing signs and omens in every day life.  The Famorians are individuals
and have no preferred alignment.

Gremlin (Air Fae)
Where doors stick and windows crack, where millstones break and armor rusts the Gremlin goes. They appear
as small, even rotund, little humanoids often with odd patches of mismatched fur and fingers that end in long
claws. They possess the wings of a bat attached to their long arms, but much of their flying ability is magical
in nature. Feared by craftsmen and homeowners alike, the Gremlin is the most destructive of all the Fae.
These mites of anarchy despise order and are drawn to destroy the goods created by others. The finer or more
complex the work, the greater their need to break it. These Fae prefer the company of each other and will run
in loose-knit clans but are otherwise uncivilized. Having sensitive eyes and skin, they do not abide light and
are essentially nocturnal. When they use weapons other than their claws they choose broken items, essentially
daggers, clubs or knives of odd shape and size. They are usually Evil in both alignment and intent.

Kenku (Hawk Avian)
These Avians are the most bird-like of all, having beaked faces and dark plumage, with white or red high-
lights, and wings partially attached to their upper limbs. It is only their hands and arms that bear resemblance
to other humanoids. They are also smaller than other Avians, averaging only three feet in height. The Kenku
is not the brightest of beings and prefers to lead a nomadic existence in family or tribal groups. It hunts by
stealth and has an unusual affinity for precious metal and gems.  The Kenku measure their status within
groups by the ostentatious jewelry they wear and thus value these goods above all. As individuals these
beings are known to be shy of larger races, however when grouped they will attack anyone they have a reason
to. The Kenku seem to get caught up in extremely bizarre forms of worship, often worshipping the elements
themselves. They also perform ritualistic arial duels for leadership that involve the falling death of the loser.
They are most often Neutral as a race.

Lamia (Cat)
Bearing the full body of a cat and the upper torso of a humanoid, they are a six-limbed, ungainly looking race.
However, they are spiritual and mystical both and are revered for their racial magical and divine talents. They
have markings that match the type of cat they emulate, whether it is tiger, panther or lion, with fur extending
onto their humanoid torsos. Their hair is remarkably human, but males tend to wear wild, almost mane-like
styles. The Lamia prefers to be near but not with others of its kind. It likes a space of its own and contact with
other members of its race but does not usually join in social orders except for yearly mating rituals and holiday
events. Yet they will work together to produce structures and edifices only to separate soon after. Some of
these structures will house small family or ruling units that rule in a general way over other Lamia. These
beings are often condescending to other races who they generally consider to be inferior. They are Neutral as a
race.

Leprechaun (Earth Fae)
These incarnations of chaos are solitary. They prefer to wear green colors to blend into forests and brushland.
They appear at first glance to be Dwarves of smaller stature and somewhat thinner build, with Elven-shaped
ears, sharper beards but rarely a moustache of any kind over their large, sensitive lips. Their greed is enormous
and they are known for gaining useless wealth, being too miserly to spend. Their gain is usually ill gotten: by
con, by stealth or just by simple thievery.  The Leprechaun is seen as a creator of luck both good and bad.
Legends also speak of their ability to grant wishes, but this is perhaps their greatest hoax.  Though they have a
proficiency for magic, few if any are powerful enough to grant wishes and even fewer would be inclined to
grant one if they could. A Leprechaun is most dangerous when their wealth is stolen; they will go to almost
any means to recover it and exact revenge for its theft. They mate only occasionally and generally avoid the
company of other beings. It is safe to say that most are Evil.

Ratahl (Rat)
The embodiment of urban peoples, the Ratahl are rodent-humanoids, standing upright and having stronger rear
limbs, but are otherwise indistinguishable from mice, vole and of course rats. They have huge communities
under or hidden in another races city. Their culture is completely parasitic and rarely creates its own goods,
preferring instead to take what it can from the surface dwellers above. The Ratahl are a night people whose
thieving skill is renown. The cities they plunder rarely discover the true source of their trouble. The Ratahl
communities are mazes of odd passageways connected to sewers, basements and warehouses.  Given their
need to escape, most ways are trapped or rigged to collapse. A Ratahl values skill above courage and result
above effort. Their kings and queens are often the most distinguished thieves among them. Most Ratahl are
Neutral but individuals of all alignments are not uncommon.

Sasquatch/Yeti (Bear)
This creature is often called the bear that walks as a man by tribal cultures. At first glance they appear to be
nothing more than a bear, but further inspection reveals that they walk erect and have much larger heads, feet,
and hands. They are a peaceful and shy people who prefer only the company of themselves in very small fami-
ly groups. They are fiercely loyal to their own and will die to save their cubs without a second thought.  The
Yeti has white fur and lives in high snowy regions, in caves and crevices, while the elusive Sasquatch is a
brown-skinned variety that is little different, but chooses the darkened forest for its home. As a race they tend
toward Neutral Good.

Satyr/Fawn (Goat)
These beings appear to have a goats furry legs and hooves, including a knee that bends opposite of most
humanoids. The males, which are properly referred to as Satyr, also sport a pair of horns that is the equal of
any goat. Most Satyr have beards or goatees, while the females, or Fawn, possess neither beard nor horns.
Their enchanting music and lilting singing is certainly the equal of most Fae.  The Satyr possess enormous
appetites for drink and for play, often involving seductions outside of their race. The Fawn is relatively shy,
but this shyness also masks playfulness the equal of any Satyr. With this reputation, many humanoid races
consider these creatures to be devilish in action and appearance. These beings prefer to live in forested and
secluded areas, away from conflicts, especially ones they start. As a race these beings tend to be Neutral in
alignment.

Seeth (Snake)
The secretive Seeth are the most arcane of the all The Wild Ones. They would say that this is a defense
against their lack of mobility, for the Seeth have no legs, relying on their snake bodies to slither slowly from
place to place. They have fine arms and hands that allow for the most delicate control and are extremely agile.
The Seeth are usually found in secret societies hidden from view of any but the slave races, most often
Saurians or even Sretts, that they use for contact with others.  Seeth mental acuity is well beyond the norm
which has provided them further gifts to protect them against the many races they consider foe.  There is no
race which trusts the Seeth and most actively seek to end any Seeth presence within their territories. They
are considered an Evil race by all.

Srett (Reptile)
Kin to the Saurian though much smaller in stature, these creatures are stealthy and move with a grace that even
the Saurian cannot match. Their diminutive size belies many of the same natural gifts of Saurians: scales,
claws, sharp teeth, and a balancing tail, which is a weapon in its own right. Whereas the Saurian relies on
strength and weight to overcome its foes, the Srett uses stealth and speed. They are more organized than
Saurians and work together without the aggressiveness of their larger kin. Srett live in and around the same
areas as Saurians and are often slaves to or, more rarely, partners with Saurians. The Srett do not possess the
fear inspiring keen of the Saurian. This race is generally Neutral in alignment.

Body Type/Chart: Humanoid

Sylph (Air Fae)
The most human appearing of any near-elf; only the tiny points of their ears and the appearance of their wings
when they fly give away their Fae nature. Like their Fairy kin, their wings are mental projections and are as
varied as the personalities of the Sylphs themselves, but unlike Fairies both sexes can form wings and fly.  The
Sylph shares ancestry with the High Elves but has taken their love of music, dancing, frivolity and pleasure to
extremes. In ages lost, this led them to discover the joy of Fairys flight. While these beings are known to be
capricious and willful, they are rarely of Evil intent. The Sylph is fiercely individualistic, but still enjoys the
company of other beings.  They have earned a reputation for playfulness that is not limited to members of
their own race, leading to many an unusual birth.

Taurian (Bull)
Arising out of legend, this warrior race is respected for strength which rivals an Ogres and stubbornness on par with a
Brownies. They appear to have an Ogres sinews with the head of a bull.  While both sexes bear horns, the females are
somewhat smaller in stature. They anger easily and fight often despite being a very honorable race. Their oral traditions
seem simple to outsiders but are layered with meaning, growing with each retelling. The Taurian respects a tale well told
almost as much as the deed itself. They perform ritualistic combat that is more sport than spectators realize, fighting for
glory alone. Only a ruling or powerful male Taurian will mate and they with as many females as possible. Taurians tend
to be Neutral Good as a race.

New Racial Abilities
Accelerated Regeneration: +4 points per 10 seconds to all
wounds; double this regeneration rate if resting.
Additional Limb Pair (arms): An additional pair of shoul-
ders, arms, forearms and hands. All are considered off-
handed.
Additional Limb Pair (legs): An additional pair of thighs,
shins and feet, removing penalty modifiers for beings nor-
mally without legs and increasing speed by 25% for those
who already possess one pair of legs.
Animal Kinship: Gains Tame Animal racial skill.
Blood Siphon: Creature can siphon blood from its victim
every 5 seconds (subtract 1d4 End. from target).
Changeling Form: Can change into another humanoid
form twice per day. The form must be within 1 1/2 feet of
height of the Changelings original form. Applying the
racial modifiers for that race, against the Changelings
original values changes physical attributes and Appearance
accordingly. The Disguise skill allows the Changeling to
assume the form of a particular individual of that race. The
Mimic skill allows the Changeling to speak as a particular
individual.
Dark Fur/Markings: +10% to Move Unseen in shadows or
low light.
Disease Carrier: Touch causes Disease Resistance or ran-
dom disease is inflicted on other races.
Entropy Touch: Crafted goods, including most weapons
and armor, suffer -1d4 damage per contact (maximum of
once per 10 seconds).  This has no effect on goods created
out of former living things (wood, leather) or goods con-
sidered already destroyed or broken.
Even Tempered: Loses Quick Temper disability and the
Berserking skill.
Fickle Luck: Each day (24 hour period) the being is either
lucky or unlucky (50/50%). 1d4+1 luck or unlucky points
(+/- 1/5%) are applied to actions the being performs that
day. GM determines which actions/effects are modified.
Fire Tolerant: Has normal Resistance to fire but takes +1
per die done.
Gift of Fire: Shares kinship with Fire Fae, +20%(more)
Magic Resistance to fire-based magic, and gains racial skill
Pyrokinesis.
Gift of Song: Gains the racial Skill Sing as well as Song
Lore if Mental attributes average 17 or greater. 
Greater Regeneration: 1d4 points are healed to all wound-
ed areas per 10 seconds. When limbs are fully healed, they
are fully grown back. Loss of vitals still causes death.
Magical Venom: The venom type is +5 levels higher and
can enter the magical range of poisons. 
Light Tolerance: Not affected by the Light intolerance
rules (applies to races who are usually light intolerant.)
Regeneration: 1d4 points are healed per minute. When
limbs are fully healed, they are fully grown back. Vital
areas still cause death.
Slow Regeneration: 1d4 points are healed per 10 minutes.
When limbs are fully healed, they are fully grown
back. Vital areas still cause death.
Stone Swimming: (3x day) per the spell of same name with
Aura points equal to Class Title x2.
Strong Changeling Form: As Changeling Form except can
change to any humanoid within 3 of the changelings
height. Also an additional +/-2 points of attributes can be
applied in addition to the racial modifiers being applied.
Sunlight Resistant: Transmutes to (type) only while struck
by sunlight. Returns to flesh afterward (applies to those
races that transmute to (type) when immersed in darkness).
Transfixing Gaze: The being stares down a single
opponent. That opponent is transfixed if less than 21 Will
Force and Will Force save fails. A new roll is allowed
every 5 seconds to break the gaze.
Treasure Sniffing (crafted): A variant of the treasure sniff-
ing ability that allows the sense of smell to be used to find
finely made goods.
Treasure Sniffing (gems, jewelry): A variant of the treasure

Very High Tolerance for Pain: -1 per die from all physical

New Racial Disabilities
Note: some abilities may appear as disabilities if the race
normally has a stronger form, i.e. Slow Regeneration is a

Animal Aversion: Loses ability to use racial skills Speak to

own. Blood loss from wounds is also subtracted from base
Endurance resulting in unconsciousness at 0 and death at 0
minus the beings Vitality.

Infertile: Inability to produce offspring.
Iron Aversion: Iron/Blessed weapons cause +1 per die

material causes 1-3 damage per contact (maximum one
damaging contact per 10 seconds).

Light Intolerance: Bright light blinds for 3-30 seconds.

Light Conversion (type): Sunlight turns the victim into the

body.

tiative.
Light Torpid: Lethargic during periods of bright light. -6
hit and damage, +2 defense, -6 initiative.
Passivity: Must choose passive as an Alignment Tendency.
Must make a Will Force save to act in conflict situations. 
No Gift of Stone: Cannot use racial skill Stone Lore.
No Locomotive Limbs: Movement is ¼ walking and no
jogging or higher movement type allowed.
No Limbs: This being possesses no limbs whatsoever, treat
as No Locomotive Limbs. The being cannot learn skills
using Strength or Agility unless possessing the skill
Telekinesis. Beings with this disability gain +20%
Telekinesis if it is an acquired skill.
No Venom: Normally poisonous being has ineffective
venom.
Obsession (type): Race is obsessed with this person, place
or thing, causing Will Force saves to avoid the pursuit of it
when presented with such opportunities.
Weak Changeling Form: As Changeling Form except can
only change to a humanoid about the same height and
frame as the Changeling.  Also can only change once per
day.
Weakness, Fire: -20% Magic Resistance to fire based
magic. Double damage is taken from all fire-based dam-
age whether magical or not.
Weakness, Cold: -20% Magic Resistance to cold based
magic. Double damage is taken from all cold-based dam-
age whether magical or not.
Worsened Fire Aversion: All fire based attacks cause triple
damage and resistance to fire based magic is -30%.

Creature to Race Conversions
From creature to playable race in ten easy stepssome creatures
can be converted to playable races with qualifications and
through the methods described below.  It should be noted that
Game Masters should nominally handle the process of making
certain creatures playable races, primarily because decisions
within the framework provided can be game-world affecting.  
Qualification:
The Creature must have at least one hand or some form of manipula-
tor at least as effective as a hand, both for spell casting, as well as
weapon wielding.
May have no attribute higher than 25.
The Creature must be able to move at least 2 miles an hour or 20 feet
per 10 seconds.
The Creature must average 8 or higher physical attributes.
The Creature must average 10 or higher mental attributes.
The Creature must average 10 or higher personal attributes.
The Creature must average 5 or higher mystical attributes.
May not have more than four magical powers or abilities (non-skill), or
must have magical powers and abilities reduced to four or less as
part of conversion to player race.
Must have a life-span of at least 5 or more years to allow class training
prior to play.
Must be able to communicate with other beings in some fashion.
Must be able to see or sense other beings.
Step 1. Racial Type 
Determine what racial classification the creature belongs to.  If it is ani-
malistic it may be a Wild One.  If it is Elven or magical it may be Fae.
Does it have elements of Earthkin, i.e. rock or mineral bones?  Use the
information below to help determine what type of race this is.
Earthkin: Mineral structure or skeleton, few magical abilities, 
Metal or Stone lore skill likely.
Fae: Long lived, magical skills, powers or abilities 
Goblinkin: Low Intelligence, high physical, short lived, brutish 
description.
Mundane: No magical powers, magical abilities, magical 
immunities or magical skills.
Wild One: Has animal abilities, natural weapons, and or 
heightened senses.
Step 2. Racial Attributes Modifiers
Creature Attribute Rating
Attribute Modifier

Notes: When all attribute modifiers are totaled there should be no more
than +9 unless the creature is lacking in some other area, i.e.  less than 4
skills, less than 4  racial classes, etc.  Even under these extreme circum-
stances it should never total more than +12.  
Endurance: Starting Modifier is determined by the following formula:
(Creature's Endurance minus Average of Physical Attributes) /
Creature's Level.  Title Bonus is 1 to 75% of base modifier (minimum
of 1) rounded up. I.e. if the new race Starting Base endurance is +10
then 1 to 8 (7.5 rounded up) or 1d8 is the Title bonus roll.

Step 5. Racial Skills 
Each skill the creature possesses is now offered to the race.  No more
than 12 skills can be available to the converted race, so remove those
over 12 that are either less relevant or that are lowest in chance.  Then
apply the modifiers shown below to each skill based on its creature
chance.
Skill Chance
Skill Bonus
1-30%
+0%
31-50%
+10%
51-75%
+20%
76-100%
+40%
101-150%
+50%
151-200%
+60%
Notes: Racial skill bonuses should never be over +60% unless the crea-
ture is lacking in some way, i.e. a negative total modifier to attributes,
very limited number of classes, etc.  In no circumstance should it exceed
+90%.  Reduce modifiers as necessary to stay within these ranges.  
Step 6. Racial Abilities and Special Abilities 
Creature abilities, whether animal or magical in nature, transfer directly
over as racial abilities.  This includes vision types, sensing types,
enhanced senses, etc.
Immunities transfer over as special abilities.  If more than four
immunties exist, additional immunities over four can be treated as pow-
ers (see below) and added to the Racial Special Ability table.  
Powers transfer as well but no more than four are available to the con-
verted race.  Each transferred power becomes a special racial ability and
is only received as part of special ability roll or GM decision for that
character.  I.e. if a race had fly at will, invisibility 3x day, blink 2x day,
finger of electricity 1x day and wall of electricity 1x day.  One power
would need to be removed.  Such as wall of electricity and the remain-
ing four powers would now become the racial special abilities table for
this creature, roll a d4 when racial special abilities are determined to
discover which special ability (power) this character had.
If the creature has no powers or immunities (or fewer than 4) to create
the racial special ability table draw 4 special racial abilities from other
existing races of the same type: Fae, Earthkin, Mundane, etc. until the
number of special racial abilities is at least 4.
Step 7. Racial Disabilities and Special Disabilities 
Creature disabilities, whether animal or magical in nature, transfer
directly over as racial disabilities.  If a creature has more than 4 disabili-
ties, choose some to add to the special racial disabilities table.
If fewer than 4 disabilities exist for the special racial disabilities table,
choose additional racial disabilities from other existing races of the
same type as the converted creature, i.e. Fae, Earthkin,  etc.
Step 8. Racial Alignment Restrictions
Based on the description given, determine the average alignment of the
converted race, if any.  If the race is individualistic, assume that a char-
acter of the converted race can be any alignment.  If a specific align-
ment is mentioned then the race should be near that Alignment.  A
Neutral creature may have individuals of Neutral Good or Neutral Evil
whereas an Evil one will not be Good.  Use the description to deduce
the societal type.
Step 9. Class Restrictions
Use the information below to restrict the races access to certain classes.
Apply all appropriate criteria.
Class Restriction by type:
Earthkin: No Mages except those which use Hermetic Lore.
Fae: No restrictions.
Goblinkin: No Seers, Sages or Mentalists.
Mundane: No restrictions.
Wild One: No Knights or Dark Knights.
Class Restriction by society:
Society
Classes available
Solitary
Warrior, Beastmaster, Rune Smith, Ranger, 
Duelist, Bandit, Scout, Druid, Rune Shaman, 
Shaman, Hermeticist, Wilder, any Witch.
Barbaric
Any Warrior save Knight or Dark Knight, Bandit, 
Scout, Druid, Rune Shaman, Shaman, 
Hermeticist, Wilder, any Witch.
Civilized
Any except Sage, Mentalist, or Seer.
Highly Civilized
No restrictions.
Class Restriction by alignment:
Good: No Dark Knights, Dark Priests, Black Witch.
Neutral: No Priests.
Evil: No Knights, Priests, White Witch.
Chaos: No Knights.
Order: No Wilder.
Moral: No Dark Knights, Black Witch.
Immoral: No Knights, Priest, White Witch.
Step 10. Finalization
Natural weapons need to be added (transferred directly from the
creature).
Natural defenses (hide). 
Subtract attribute, weight and size modifiers from creature combat
statistic to produce any racial combat bonuses.  
List life-span and starting age at the onset of maturity for the race.
If the race is barbaric or solitary, create a simple list of starting
equipment by highest social class to lowest and note the race does not
get starting money.  Use the starting equipment lists for barbaric
(Barbaric Human, Mountain Dwarf) and solitary (Ogre, Troll) as
templates.
Determine movement modifiers for race by taking the races average
Agility (13 + modifer) and comparing the movement rates listed for that
Agility with the Creatures actual. The difference is the racial movement
modifier.
Jumping is determined by general type the creature is closest to: Elf,
Ogre, etc.

Race to Creature Conversions
Early Imagine play often involves using the diverse races themselves as
opponents for the player characters. The conversion from race to crea-
ture is a simple process of determining a few statistics from the race
itself. The step by step guide is outlined below.
Creature Type: Creature type is based on the racial category.
Earthkin: Magical Humanoid.
Fae: Magical Humanoid.
Goblinkin: Humanoid, Magical Humanoid (if having any 
magic abilities, powers, skills or immunities).
Mundane: Humanoid.
Wild One: Humanoid, Magical Humanoid (if having any 
magic abilities, powers, skills or immunities).
Creature Level: Determine creature level by using the Creature Creation
system in the Masters Manual.
Attributes: Creature attributes are set at 13 and then Racial Attribute
modifiers are added. Racial attribute maximums are consulted to deter-
mine if the creature has exceeded in attribute and that attribute is low-
ered to the maximum allowed. 
Resistances:

Endurance: Average physical attributes + racial starting modifier.
Height/Length: Use the average height for that race.
Weight: Use the average weight for the average frame for that race.
Movement: Use average Agility (13 + Modifer), + racial movement
modifiers.
Combat Modifiers: Based on attributes, size, weight and special racial
abilities.
Alignment: Use information provided by racial description. If none is
indicated, assume Neutral.
Attack Skill: Is determined by combining average physical and mental
ratings into a single number.

Skills: All skills are determined by the Racial Skills listed. Determine

Powers:  These are taken from all racial abilities and all special abilities
that are spell-like.
Abilities: Non-spell like special abilities, special senses, or enhanced
senses. Provide the creature version of a race with any of the races spe-
cial abilities on the special racial ability table that seem applicable to
define the race.
Disabilities: Add all racial disabilities and any special racial disabilities
that would define the race from the special racial disabilities table.
Immunities: All racial immunities are creature immunities as well.

Gargoyle

===== PAGE 32 =====
24
To his grace, the Lord Baron Pitor Dugas, 
I thank you for this honor, though I think myself unworthy of the task you have set before me. Nevertheless, as you have asked and the
Ringleader has commanded, I will present to you a catalogue of the beasts I have encountered and their particulars, as well as my own story, or at
least what there is of it.
As for the latter of the two , I could simply say I was born, came to my current profession and here I am, or I could offer you such detail, it
would fill up the pages of a novel. Instead, I'll just speak something a little more brief and hope you find it to your liking.
As you are no doubt aware, I was born in one of your own freeholds, the son of Knight Baronett Mitigus Greenbriar. I was his second son, and
therefore not under the strict burden to follow in his footsteps. While this did offer me much time to pursue my own interests, it also left me with
the listlessness that comes from being full of youth and vigor with little to direct it towards. I still learned many of the gentle skills that come with
being in the household of a minor nobleman, not the least of which was riding. 
I was often chided for the many hours devoted to the saddle.  I would take any excuse to ride, be it to practice the lance, running a message to a
fellow Lord's household or simply excusing myself to enjoy the sunset.   I felt the need to roam far and fast, though I could never say where or
why. Another symptom of being young, perhaps, but if so, I must still be young for it has never left me. 
No well traveled road was on our lands, so we seldom had visitors. When we did, it was usually a small matter of business or a passerby who
had lost his way. You can imagine my amazement when word came one day that not a single visitor or carriage was coming to visit us, but instead,
a whole caravan!  I could not conceive of it. The whole house was excited and finally my parents disclosed it was a surprise, a treat for all of us,
arranged by your good father, still Baron at that time.  There was to be a circus, with two rings, performers and beasts of all kinds.
When it came, I could scarcely keep myself from bursting. I pulled myself away from my lessons that day, even though I knew it would cost me
later, and watched as they took that year's fallow field and erected a sprawling village in a matter of hours. I saw how they worked, the men and the
creatures who both performed and labored with their two legged counterparts. I managed to sneak closer until I walked among the tents and cages
and wagons, jittery with excitement. And then, I was amongst the cats: fierce, beautiful, graceful, terrifying, powerful, the stuff of both dream and
nightmare. I was in awe. That was when I felt a hand upon my shoulder and nearly lept off my own skeleton.
There, next to me, was a gruff man with sun beaten skin, an iron grey moustache, salt and pepper hair, a hooked nose and a fierce scowl accent-
ed by the peaks in his bushy eyebrows. I stood shock still. His hand was powerful and calloused, his arms thick and firm with muscle like braided
cord. Scars great and small stood out light against the dun of his complexion and a whip hung at his side. He asked me what I thought I was doing,
and I could barely squeak out an apology for whatever crime I'd committed. He snorted and smirked, then looked into my eyes hard. 
"A little boy has no place here." He spoke in clipped, measured words.
"I'm not a little boy," I answered, my 11 year old voice cracking, though not yet from puberty. 
He stared into my eyes for a long time before turning me around and holding me so I could not help but gaze into the eyes of a huge striped
tiger.
"See there?" he said, "There is death with legs, boy. It could swallow you up in a single bite, and feel none the more full for it. It's a predator
and a killer. It's power, boy, a power you wouldn't understand. So go home to your books and fine clothes, before you have to face what's before
you."
I heard him but his words didn't frighten me. Instead my wonder was aroused, and I was invigorated by the things he said. I felt alive in the
presence of so great a beast, like the sensation I felt when I had my favorite stallion at full gallop and the wind was rushing at my face and I could
feel the muscle of the horse beneath me and for just a moment, a moment, we were one and I grew by the horse's power.  He was right to think I
didn't understand it then, but I wanted to, and I think resolved to do just that, if it took the rest of my life.
I went home in a daze, and then waited impatiently for the next night, when we would go see the circus in full.
The performance was amazing. The performers were fine; the clowns were gleeful; the dare-devils were captivating, but my heart raced only
when the animal acts came on. And the man who had found me, he was, of course, the lion tamer. 
I could think of nothing else for the next day, my mind able to focus only on fangs and fur and hooves and claws and tusks and mighty roars,
growls and trumpeting. I snuck back to the edge of the circus tents and watched. When I returned I was chided and my father said to stay away. We
went again to the second performance, and it thrilled me more than the first. I tried to sneak away again the next day and was caught by my father.
He punished me, not allowing me to attend the third and final show.  I could find no way to escape my sentence. I wept that night, to the sounds of
the circus off in the distance, and slept.
I tried to concentrate on my work the next day and not think of the circus packing up and leaving, but it was no use. I had to understand the
magic in beasts, how their handlers understood and guided them so. I did not sleep that night but instead came to a plan. My father would never
approve, so I did not ask; I simply acted. I ran away to the circus.
It took me two days to catch them, and when I did, the lion tamer was the one to greet me. He simply asked what had kept me and gave me a
bed and some food.
It has been 8 years of hard work and training since then, but I am happy with what I have become. My father and I have made peace, and my
teacher, the lion tamer, has retired and left me in his place. In addition to that task, I have assisted my fellows in the capture of new beasts for our
attractions, as well as studied the ones in our care. And what I have learned, I now pass on to you, Baron. I hope you find my efforts satisfactory.
Sincerely,
Anivar Greenbriar, Beastmaster with the Welgus Two Ring Circus

Beastmaster (Warrior subclass)
From the bare-chested, vine-swinging, jungle native to the finely dressed nobleman trainer, the Beastmaster is a formidable figure.
Combining the cunning of the slyest animal, the ruthlessness of a sentient species, and the martial discipline of a trained warrior,
the Beastmaster captures the title king of the animals.  From the confines of a breeding pen or the engulfing jungle canopy, the
Beastmaster dominates wild animals through training, force of will and a touch of divine magic.  A telepathic-like bond exists
between the handler and the animal kingdom, from the land-locked beasts of burden to the lofty raptors to the abominations that
inhabit the deep blue.  While the animal within us all is buried deeply, the Beastmaster is adept in conjuring forth and controlling
this primordial spirit.  Eventually, even the physical manifestation of the spirit is adopted by the Beastmaster in place of his own. 
Alignment Requirements: None.

===== PAGE 34 =====
26
I think the swamp is alive.  I don't mean it's teeming with life. I don't mean it's full of vegetation. I mean it's alive, with a
mind and it's trying to kill me.
When I and the King's Expeditionary detachment arrived, this was never in the plan. Observe. Report. Simple
enough. But then things happened. Terrible things. Splashes in the water. Hisses on the dock. My companions torn limb
from limb. The swamp was dangerous, this we knew, but weren't prepared to go to war with it. It was ready to swallow
us and forget the bones.
I'm writing this to stay sane. I'm not a man of many fine words, and I know no stories, so all I can do is write
down what I know.  I know wildlife. I know flowers. I know horrors and beautiful things. I know nature.
As a boy, all I thought about was adventure. If only I knew then, eh? Adventure meant risk, so when the King's
men came about, looking for volunteers to join the Expeditionary Corps, I jumped. To wander! To be part of the
Kingdom's elite! What else was there? My mother protested, but my father thought it would be good to put some miles
under my feet to maybe get some wanderlust out of my system before I settled down and produced some grandchildren.
It took me only a few minutes to gather a pack and some food. I was off.
Becoming a scout was easy, as I grew up on an outskirt farm. The skills were already there, I just needed to
hone them. I entered into the service when I was fourteen and was allowed on my first real outing two years later.  At
first, it was just wonderful to be away from home. Then it was good to learn things. Then it was work, but there was
always more to learn and learning was joy. I enjoyed my apprenticeship.
Since then I've learned so many things. Some were wonderful, others were hideous. My travels initially took me
to safer places, just beyond the borders. Things I'd only heard my master speak of in lecture around a dying fire were
now small threads that held my life in place.  A detail forgotten and I might be a meal or corpse. Mistake a serrated leaf
for a smooth one, I might make myself sick for days. Nature, she is an odd lass.
A pretty thing can hold danger. Ugly things can be harmless. There can be glory in both. We are all a part of her,
but few understand her. We try to get away from nature, with walls and cities and perfumes, but she is always there, as
disease or dinner, friend or foe. Nature is companion, master, enemy, or saviour, depending on the day. I've worshiped
her and cursed her.  Nature is a harsh mistress, but she and my master reared what child was left in me well.
This was my first mission into the deep uncharted lands.
And I don't want to go out like this.
Three weeks ago, it began. First to fall was one of the archers, followed by Toftin, the physiquer. At first I
thought it was just tragic carelessness, but I now think it may be orchestrated, that there's a plan to what has happened to
my seven companions. Why it has chosen to leave me for last, I do not know. But here I am.
My master, Sergeant Fex Wiskin, is gone. He told me never to write down what he taught. "The pen will make
you weak-minded, boy," he said. But now, the pen is the only voice I have. Paper is my only companion left. I hope
someone will find this journal, and maybe find what has done this to us. Maybe it is my last chance to live on in some-
one's memory.
May someone find this, so our trip was not completely in vain...
-Recovered from the Journal of the Scout Harkis Vohl, King's Expeditionary Corps, presumed dead.

Border Scout (Rogue subclass)
The Border Scout is adept at getting deep into wilderness areas and gaining valuable information that can be used by military com-
manders and fellow adventurers alike.  The Border Scout can move ahead into dangerous territory without being spotted, find out
what he needs to know, and get out while never being detected.  Later he acquires magical skills which allow him to gain even more
information, such as the ability to detect supernatural and undead creatures from a safe distance.  The Border Scout does not wish to
get too close to the enemy.  For this reason, they avoid close combat and prefer to use missile weapons when they must eliminate an
enemy.  A scout who engages in hand to hand combat has gotten far too close to the enemy and often will not survive long if he
cannot escape.  For this reason, Border Scouts have a very dangerous job indeed.
Alignment Requirements: None.
Attribute Requirements: Agility 13, Intelligence 13, Knowledge 13.
Special Modifiers: +30% to core skills, +10% to stealth/intrusive skills, +5% to informational skills, +5% to combat skills.

===== PAGE 36 =====
28
The name given to me at birth is Pock, which means swift. I am of the Togis tribe, the son of Coag, the former shaman
and rune-master of my people. To those I met in my travels, I introduced myself Coag'in Pock, meaning 'The swift son
of Coag'.  Family is a very important aspect to my people, the Grahl.
From the time I could make sounds, my father taught me. He was neither especially stern, nor did he ever let me
forget the importance of what I was to learn. I studied each lesson diligently, my younger brother joining me after a year.
Each new wisdom I gained lit a fire in my heart. First I was to learn language, then names and then the sigils that would
not only represent a word, but call forth its virtue. I learned to draw and sing, and each night we kept the Vigil of
Sundown, singing the world to slumber and thanking the sun for making the day's efforts possible.
Our time was not only spent sitting on logs and making lines in wood and sand. My tribe travels the length of
the plains lands and forests, going to the large farms and offering our backs to bring in the harvest. I worked with Father
and Mother, my brother and my two sisters, slinging the sickle and singing the harvest songs that helped us keep pace.
We would gather corn in one place, travel for a month, unearth potatoes, move again to gather wheat. It was a fine time
and one that helped reinforce the cycle of things.  Other such lessons were not so pleasant.
In my seventh autumn, near my eighth birthday, Father, my brother and I were at the camp's edge, keeping the
Vigil. The wind was chill and the air smelled foul, of death left to rot. Father sensed it.  My brother sensed something,
though he knew not what. Had any of us known for certain, though, I do not believe Father would have abandoned the
rite. But even as we sung the words and pronounced the blessings, we all trembled. Then it struck.
To see a Manticore pounce is a fearsome sight. Father was mauled and helpless in less than a breath. My brother
froze though I ran screaming back to camp.  Spear carriers came from their tents to fight this force of nature. Swords
and bows swung and twanged and clanked in the growing night. When it was over, more than 15 of my tribe were
killed, Father first amongst them. My brother was mauled, but alive. Mother had not been one to raise a weapon, pulling
my brother from the fray; instead only to fall with one of the creatures quills in her back.  She would not see another
three sunsets. My sisters lived, thankfully.
The beast itself was not killed, but instead flew away, taking two bodies to feed its foul hungers. My kinsman's
efforts were not totally in vain, but left only one deep scar and blinded one eye of the monster. This would serve me in
later years, but no one felt victorious that night.
My sisters and I sung the songs of the dead, and kept watch over my brother until the fear of fever had passed.
Despite the fact I was only barely able to lift my father's runed sword, I set out to find the creature who had done this
and take revenge.  The rest of my tribe moved on, and I left my sisters and one uncle to see to my brother. I walked the
first steps of my journey in tears and in fear that I had somehow been cowardly, though I knew I could have done noth-
ing. But one day, I would find the beast, and that day I would be able to do something. I would make retribution.
I tracked the monster for eleven years. It flew so far north that I did not see seven days in that land without
snow.  So far south I had to follow it that I thought I have to shave the whole of my fur to keep cool.  During my travels,
I traversed every terrain and region our land has to offer. I saw many wondrous things, as well as many disturbing and
frightful. I continued in my studies, drawing on the words Father, still present in my heart despite the gap of years. And I
grew in wisdom.
I shall not make long of the end my journeys, but only say the Manticore was taken by my hand alone, though I
did not walk away without reminders of its fearsome nature. Once I healed, and spake my peace with the Sun and with
Father, now in Lands of the eternal sun, I came home and took a wife and fathered a son.
And so it is to you, my son, I leave this book of my travels and of the the things and creatures I found there. Use
it to bring wisdom to our people.
-Pock, Rune Shaman

Rune Shaman (Priest subclass)
In an age before Mages learned to direct Aura magic into spells, when natural substances and runes contained the only known Aura
power, the craft of the Rune Shaman was greatly valued and passed from generation to generation.  The Rune Shaman is one who is
trained in the ancient arts of runes, and has the ability to harness the Aura within himself.  Relying on runes and rituals to augment
his fighting prowess, he is a fierce opponent in battle.  As master of the oldest lore, he is also adept at the animal and plant skills con-
necting him to the animal and plant spirits of the wild.  Traditionally, he was dependent on the ability to understand and release the
magic found within items of power, as well as the secret power locked away in the stones, woods and metals of the world.

Where does life reside?
That question, so insidiously simple, has plagued me my entire life. I mean, the common wisdom is the
mind does the thinking, and there's the pesky notion of the soul, but if I think "hand open", the appendage
does not necessarily obey.  If I wish my hand to obey, there need be no such specific cognition, I simply will
it. Is the will, then, part of soul? I think perhaps. But then, the question: where is it?
If people perished only from wounds, it would be sufficient. Obviously, certain parts are required to
pump blood hither and yon, to keep food and waste in their proper place, (note to self: must work on veg-
etable/animal fusions further, replace food with sunlight), and when these organs are damaged, they cease to
do their job, and something goes horribly wrong, and you have a corpse. But why are some wounds mortal
and others not? And why do some perish without violence? What is this thing called life or essence or the soul
that animates crude matter that we call animal or vegetable or man? (Note: compare live tissue and dead tissue
for weight.)
Well, someone must know. This is not to say that someone does, only that the knowing and understand-
ing of this principle must occur, and I am suitable, perhaps, I write in all humility, even more so. There is the
reason for my research. (note: Odd word that, re-search. Often one is trying to find a thing in the first place...
should it not simply be search? hmm, too much digression...) The obvious question then, a question I posed to
myself while under Doctor Pheston, is how does one go about said research?
To me, and this is perhaps why I am most fit to possess this knowledge, hidden under the flesh and in
obscure corners of the mind by strange gods, (note: devise method for studying divine corpus. Dead god
required?) it is perfectly clear the method required. Let those fools in the Guild criticize and then go about
their philosophies. Let them ponder and limit themselves to cursory exams of long dead tissue. I seek answers,
damn them! Not ephemoral platitudes and semi-religious creation stories! Action is required, direct action.
And so, I go to the source. Live animals. Disections. Study of animate vs. inanimate.
Perhaps one would call me successful for the findings to which I've already come (note: where did I
leave those vials of invisibility salve? Cabinet?) but I will not rest until this conundrum is solved. What is life,
and where can it be found in a body?
So, in this notebook will I record my findings with specimens both currently under observation and
those I intend to capture. 
Step one: Acquire. 
Step two: Examine thoroughly. 
Step three: Record Ecology. 
Step four: Exterminate. 
Repeat steps two through four.
-Turgis Weldon, Hermeticist

Hermeticist (Mage subclass)
He is a hermit who probably learned his skills from a teacher or parent who was formally trained as a mage but was forced to leave
civilization.  In the harsh lands where he grew up many of the formal studies of the mage were neglected for basic survival skills.
This mage avoids people, and has learned to work with the animals, both for food and as allies in order to survive.  He often has
only a limited number of spells and is forced to depend more on his skills.  He sets traps for animals and intruders.  He can be
aggressive and will attack intruders to protect his home.

Introduction to the Creatures
This book contains the creatures and beasts that characters
may meet in the wilderness, in the forests, swamps, bogs
and plains.   It offers aids to the Game Master and player
alike.
GM Notes for Creatures
Creature Statistics
Each Creature has its own set of numbers used for
combat. These numbers are considered to be the aver-
age for that creature type, and can be modified
according to situation.  It is not recommended that
you deviate an attribute more than 2 or 3 points in
either direction, nor that you change Endurance more
than 10 to 30 points in either direction. In any case,
this allows the GM to modify encounters and keep the
creatures variable. In some cases, a creature will not
have all of the statistical information listed with it.  In
these instances refer to the first creature in the section
for the remaining stats.  
Lore and Informational Skills
Various Lore skills allow characters to gain knowl-
edge about animals, herbs, creatures, etc.  The best
way to implement this as a GM is to allow a player
who makes an appropriate Lore roll to open the
Aspects of the Wild book and access the information
directly.  Other players should not be allowed to do
this during game play.
Example: A character with Bestiary Lore wants to do
research on the Wyvern. He goes to a library and
spends the correct amount of time, then makes a roll.
If successful, the GM allows him to open and access
the Wyvern in this book whenever he wishes. The
secondary use of these skills involves gaining infor-
mation on the spot during an encounter, and the above
method should not be used for these type of rolls, but
instead the GM should give out bits of information as
he sees fit.  
Examples of such skills that apply : Bestiary Lore,
Animal Lore, Herb Lore. These skills, explained in
the Players Guide, can be used to identify: Hermetic
Lore, Poison Lore, Potion Lore.  Identification allows
the character to know if any parts of the creature are
used in potions or recipes.
Character Uses for Creatures
Creature Mounts and Pets
Many of the creatures in this book can be used as
character mounts.  The rules for obtaining and con-
trolling such creatures can be found within the rules
of the Players Guide and Masters Manual. Mounts
and pets can add great flavor and entertainment to the
game, and a GM should be expecting his players to
attempt to acquire them.
Evoke
Any creature of type Animal may be evoked with the
skill Evoke, but Magical Animals may not. Every ani-
mal in this book has an Evoke Package included with
it, in case any characters wish to have that animal as
their totem, and to allow the GM to create interesting
and amusing GMCs with unusual totems.  
New Evoke Packages
These are special Evoke packages that emulate trees
and plants as opposed to animals. 
Tree
Barkhide
Endurance
Force of Wood
Instinct (water)
Light Regeneration
Rooted
Strength

New Tree Evoke abilities:
Barkhide: 1 point of hide per 5 Endurance, -1 appear-
ance per 5 points of hide.
Force of Wood: +30% to the Force skill.  Can also
cause 1 point of structural damage per minute.
Light Regeneration: Regeneration of 1 point of dam-
age, per 10 minutes in full sunlight, to all wounded
areas.
Rooted: +20% to Brace skill and any saves or con-
tests involving agility or strength against being moved
or toppled, cannot move while this ability is evoked
and cannot be evoked over stone, metal or similar
hard surface.
Tree Merge: Can join with any tree, losing current
form and become one with the tree, dying only when
the tree is killed. Can leave the tree at any time. 
Tree Walk: Can enter any tree and then appear in any
other tree in the same forest. A forest is defined as
any group of trees where no tree is more than 100 feet
from the next.
Totem Call
The skill Totem Call allows for the character to sum-
mon forth an incarnation of his totem animal, and
these should always be considered type Magical
Animal for purposes of spells and effects.
Animal Totems
Any character who chooses a character connected to
the natural world will most likely need to choose a
totem animal to use with certain skills, and for role-
playing flavor in general.  The following list gives
sample totems and what they often represent, and
should help a player choose a totem appropriate to his
character concept.
Ant: group mind, patience, action
Alligator: maternal, revenge
Antelope: action, agility, sacrifice
Badger: aggressive, healer, energy conduit
Bat: rebirth, secrets and initiation, long life
Bear: power, industrious, instinct, healing
Beaver: determined, strong-willed, builder, protector 
Bee: organization, industrial, productive, wisdom
Buffalo: sacredness, life
Butterfly: metamorphosis, transformation
Cheetah: swiftness, insight, focus
Coyote: trickery, intelligence, stealth
Crow: law, change, creativity, spiritual strength,
energy
Deer: intelligence, gentleness, caring, kindness
Dog: noble, faithfulness, loyal, teaching
Dragonfly: flighty, carefree
Eagle: divine spirit, sacrifice, connection to creator
Elephant: strength, power, wisdom
Elk: strength, agility
Falcon: new beginnings, adventure, passion, 
leadership
Fox: cunning, agile, quick-witted
Frog: water energy, cleansing, rebirth
Gazelle: the soul, aggressive
Hawk: messenger, intuition, victory, healing
Horse: stamina, mobility
Jaguar: chaos, shape-shifter
Lion: family, strength, energy, courage
Lizard: conservation
Llama: comforting others
Lynx: keeper of secrets, guardian, guide
Moose: headstrong, longevity, steadfastness, wisdom
Mouse: scrutiny, order, organizer
Oppossum: diversion, strategist, deceiver
Otter: playful, friendly, dynamic
Owl: deception, clairvoyance, insight, messenger
Ox: sacrifice, self-denial, chastity
Porcupine: innocence, companionship, trust
Rabbit: fear, timidity, nervousness, humility
Raven: introspection, courage, self-knowledge
Skunk: reputation, presence, strength

Squirrel: planner, gatherer

Turtle: nurturer, protector, mother energy
Weasel: strength, energy, ingenuity, stealth
Wolf: loyalty, perserverance, success

Body Charts
Each table describes the body areas and the modifier to base Endurance
for the specific areas, whether a limb or a vital region, the effect upon
loss of the area and finally any chance for special injury effects.
Choose the body area which is most appropriate to the size and shape
of the creature.  In some cases you may even need to create a new body
type.
Tiny
Creatures less than about a foot in average length or height have the
tiny body type.  These creatures typically have 2 or fewer Endurance
regardless of the makeup of their body structure, although they may
have more through magical means.  They are considered to have one
body area, and must be struck with a hit to the center target region in
the attack charts (see Player's Guide).  Since nearly any blow will
destroy the body of such a creature, vital areas do not produce addition-
al chances to incapacitate. 
Area
End
Type
Effect
Special Effect
Entire Body
x1
Vital
Death
None
Small
Creatures over a foot but less than three feet in average length or height
have a small body type.  These creatures will usually have 10 or less
Endurance regardless of the structure of their body unless the creature
is magical. These creatures are considered to have one body area, but it
may be struck by any hit on the attack chart (see Player's Guide). Each
blow has a 1% chance per point of damage taken to cause an incapaci-
tating or disabling blow to the creature. Of these blows, 50% are said to
strike a limb and 50% are said to strike a vital area. Vital hits can cause
incapacitation for 4d6 seconds. Limb hits can disable the limb, slowing
movement and removing usage of the limb until healed.

shape which is composed of sections which are all equal.  These crea-
tures may have any number of vital body areas and any number of non-
vital areas, depending on the power of the creature.  This is determined
from the magical abilities section.

Mollusk
Mollusks have soft bodies which are often enclosed in a hard shell.  They have
a muscular foot which in some cases (such as the octopus) has developed into
multiple limbs.  Mollusks include slugs, snails, octopi, squid, clam and oysters.

Hybrid
Any mixture of the above body types can be created by pulling the appropriate
body regions out from each chart and combining them.  In such cases, a new
chart must be made by pulling groups of areas from the appropriate body charts.
For example, a new creature might be a quadruped/reptile hybrid.  This creature
might have items from the quadruped: Head, neck, forequarters,right foreleg,
left foreleg, right forefoot, left forefoot;and others from the reptile:
Hindquarters, underbelly, right hindleg, left hindleg, right hindclaw, left hind-
claw, upper tail, lower tail.
New Magical Abilities
Gaze Effects
Special powers or attacks that require gaze occur when the gaze
of the creature meets the gaze of a humanoid or another crea-
ture directly.  A normal mirror or other indirect viewing device
does not transmit the power of the gaze.  A magical mirror
(Mirror Maze spell) will reflect a gaze attack.  Scrying or simi-
lar viewing can cause the gaze effect to manifest depending on
the viewing angle.  Gaze effects that transform or alter are
afforded a Magic Resistance to avoid the effects.  Those effects
that control or alter the mind allow a Control Resistance to be
avoided.  No individual is required to save more than twice per
10 seconds regardless of the number of times gaze is met.
Magical effects are permanent until a Transmute to Flesh spell
is used.  Control effects last 10 minutes per level of the creature
unless otherwise noted.
Breath Attacks
These emanate from the mouths of the creatures who possess
them.  They come in several varieties, namely gas clouds, bolts,
cones and spit globs whose dimensions and strengths are cate-
gorized into weak and strong types.  These attacks can not be
attempted for 10 seconds after the last attempt. Poisonous
breath attacks require a Poison Resistance to avoid.  All others
require a Magic Resistance to avoid their effects.  Damage and
other effects are provided with the creature.
Blinding Poison
This new type of poison may have any standard potency, but
has a new effect for its Type.  The effect is to cause blindness
for 2d12 hours if a Poison Resistance is failed.
Attack Types
Weak Gas Cloud: This attack creates an oval cloud 2' long and
1' wide (and deep) per 20 Endurance of the creature with a 100'
by 50' by 50' maximum cloud size. The cloud may only be cre-
ated 3 times per day.  Roll of 10 or better (plus all missile modi-
fier) on a d20 indicates correct placement.  Otherwise cloud is
off by 1d4 feet per 5' of the cloud; placement is off in the direc-
tion indicated by a d12. 
Strong Gas Cloud: This breath emanates as an oval cloud 2'
long and 1' wide (and deep) per 10 Endurance of the creature
with a 200' by 100' by 100' maximum size. This breath attack
may only be used 3 times per hour. Roll of 10 or better (plus all
missile modifier) on a d20 indicates correct placement.
Otherwise cloud is off by 1d6 feet per 5' of the cloud; place-
ment is off in the direction indicated by a d12. 
Weak Bolt: This attack extends in a line directly from the
mouth, traveling for 2' per 20 Endurance of the creature with a
100' maximum.  The width of the bolt is only a few inches
across and strikes only one body area on most human sized tar-
gets.  This attack can only be attempted 3 times per day.
Normal attack roll is used to strike opponents.
Strong Bolt: This attack travels in a line directly from the crea-
ture's mouth, with a range of l' per 20 Endurance of the creature
and a 200' maximum it is 6" in diameter. This attack can be
attempted 3 times per hour. Normal attack roll is used to strike
opponents.  However, opponents directly in line of the bolt all
must save as the bolt will extend through each opponent until
all damage has been absorbed or the maximum length has been
reached.
Weak Cone: The cone produced by this attack is 2' long per 20
Endurance of the creature with a 100' maximum length.  The
base is 1/4 width of the length with a maximum of 25'.  The
cone can target a humanoid with an attack rolled on the stan-
dard attack chart.  A miss indicateds the cone has failed to hit
the being and the edge of the cone is placed next to the being.
An off center hit indicates that the cone has struck the individ-
ual but that one edge of the cone is placed directly on the indi-
vidual.  A dead center hit places the cone directly on the target-

Strong Cone: This attack produces a cone 2' long per 10
Endurance of the creature with a 200' maximum length.  The
base is 1/4 width of the length with a maximum of 50'.  The
cone can be aimed at any humanoid sized target with an attack
roll. A miss indicates the cone has completely missed the target,
so the edge of the cone is placed next to the being.  A near hit
indicates that the cone has struck the individual, so one edge of
the cone is placed directly on the individual.  A dead center hit
places the cone directly on the targeted being.  Alternately, the
cone can be placed without targeting an individual.  In these
cases the cone is placed with a 10 or better on a d20 after adding
all missile modifiers.  A miss indicates the cone is off in place-
ment by 1d4 per 5' of the width of the base.
Weak Spit Glob: A glob of spit is fired from the creature's
mouth. Can travel up to 3' per 20 Endurance of the creature with
a 150' maximum.  The glob is about a 6" to 1'6" in average
diameter and will strike one humanoid body region on a normal
sized target.  The normal attack chart is used to strike opponents.
Range is considered short until 1/2 of the maximum distance and
medium thereafter. This attack can be used up to 3 times in a
single day.
Strong Spit Glob: This attack projects a spit glob from the crea-
ture that travels up to 3' per 10 Endurance of the creature with a
maximum of 300'.  The glob is about 2' to 4' in size and strikes 1
humanoid body region and two random adjacent ones.  Range is
short up to 1/2 of the maximum range and medium for the
remaining distance. The attack chart is used to hit.  This attack
can be utilized 3 times per hour.
New Diseases
White Death (Rabies)
Type: Brain
Agent: Virus in saliva transmitted by animal(humanoid) bite
Incubation period: 1-3 days
Effects
Mild: There are no mild cases in humanoids. In animals a mild
case causes -1 to Intelligence and makes it a disease carrier.
Serious (01-20%): 1-3 weeks. Apprehension, nervousness, anxi-
ety, solitude and a fever. Will Force save is required to concen-
trate. Weakness during fever periods: -2 physical, -2 mental.
+25% to Berserking
Terminal (21-100%): 1-4 weeks. Paranoia, fatigue, disorienta-
tion, seizures and death. Inability to concentrate. -4 mental -2
physical until death. +50% to Berserking, 2 levels of fatigue dur-
ing the illness.
Recurring: 15%
Black Death (Bubonic Plague)
Type: Lung/Skin
Agent: Bacterium in fleas found on rodents (or any small
animal) living in unsanitary conditions. 
Incubation period: 3d4 days
Effects
Mild (01-20%): 2d4 weeks. Fever, coughing, weakness. -2 phys-
ical, -1 mental. 
Serious (21-80%): 3d4 weeks. High Fever, coughing blood,
extreme weakness, pustules can form on the skin. -4 physical -2
mental, -3 Appearance. 2 levels of fatigue during the illness.
Vitality save or lose 1 point of Vitality and 2 points of
Endurance permanently.
Terminal (81-100%): 4d4 weeks. Extreme Fever, coughing
blood, shortness of breath, pustules usually form on the skin,
death. -6 physical -4 mental, -4 Appearance. 4 levels of fatigue
during the illness.
Recurring: 30%
Yellow Fever (Malaria)
Type: Blood/Liver
Agent: Bacterium in bite of an infected insect (mosquito) living
in a warm wet climate. 
Incubation period: 1-3(+1) weeks
Effects
Mild (01-20%): 1d4 months. Fever, chills, vomiting, weakness
and jaundice. -2 physical, -1 mental, -1 Appearance. 1 level of
fatigue during illness.
Serious (21-80%): 2d4 months. High fever, vomiting, diarrhea,
dehydration, extreme fatigue and jaundice, -4 physical -2 mental,
-2 Appearance. 4 levels of fatigue during the illness. Vitality
save or lose 1 point of Vitality and 3 points of Endurance perma-
nently.
Terminal (81-100%): 4d4 months. vomiting, diarrhea, extreme
dehydration, extreme fatigue and jaundice, death. 1/2 physical 
-4 mental, -3 Appearance. 6 levels of fatigue during the illness.
Recurring: 20%
The Pox (Small Pox)
Type: Skin/Circulatory System
Agent: Airborn/contact with infected humanoids. 
Incubation period: 1-2 (+1) days
Effects
Mild (01-20%): 3d4 days. Fatigue, fever, rigors, headache, and

save or lose 1 point of Appearance permanently.
Serious (21-80%): 4d4 days. Extreme fatigue, high fever, rigors,

and extremities), death. -4 physical -2 mental, -6 Appearance. 5
levels of fatigue during the illness. 
Recurring: 40%

Alligator (Animal, level 3)
We have a fellow who likes to wrestle these things. He and I
go drinking and compare notes on our respective acts. I asked
him once how he got started, and he pointed out a number of
pantheons with crocodile or alligator gods. He told me it was
like wrestling with immortality itself.... -Anivar
Habits/Life Cycle: The alligator is found in freshwater swamps
and slow-moving waterways.  The crocodile can be found in
fresh or salt water.  Both types prefer dark or murky waterways.
Both species prefer to hunt at night and relax in the heat of the
day.  These creatures eat a wide variety of animals.  Small ones
feed on insects, crayfish, small fish and frogs.  Adults eat larger
fish, water birds, turtles and various small mammals and occa-
sionally consume larger mammals and such domestic animals as
dogs and hogs.  They rarely attack humanoids.  They can live to
50 years.
Climate/Habitat: Humid Continental, Humid Oceanic, Humid
Subtropical, Subtropical Dry Summer, Tropical Wet and
Tropical Wet and Dry. River, ocean (surface), swamp, marsh,
rainforest and jungle.
Body Type: Giant (reptile)

rolled to hit, the opponent is gripped.  Thereafter, the victim
cannot escape without a contest of strength against the animal.)
Water Roll (5 seconds, if gripped and in water, a contest of
strength can ensue instead of the usual attack.  If the alligators
opponent loses the contest she is rolled and pulled underwater.
Drowning begins and will occur at twice the normal speed.
Once the being is rolled, the prey will be gripped or further
rolled until dead or escaped.
Attack Notes:  Prefers to surprise from water, attack until grip,
then pull under the water and water roll the prey until dead.
